result
1	# Threat Model
2	
3	Owner: Marcus Ilori (`02-ai-security-architecture`). Scope per this track's `CLAUDE.md`: credential storage, PII exposure, and third-party `access_token` handling. Retention/lifecycle policy is `../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`'s call, not mine — I model exposure and access risk, not how long data should live.
4	
5	## Assumptions, named explicitly
6	
7	1. **Assets in scope**: `user_credential` (username, method, password — modeled by Priya/`05-data-ops` as a lookup-table `method`, not a native enum, so a given user can have more than one credential row, e.g. password + WebAuthn), `user_profile` (name, address, phone), and third-party `access_token`s obtained via the IDP connector's `/auth`.
8	2. **`method` is not "always password."** Priya's schema treats `method` as an FK into an `auth_method` table (`id`, `name`, `requires_secret`, `is_active`). That means "credential storage" is not one hashing problem — it's a family of them, one per method, and this document treats it that way rather than assuming a single password column.
9	
10	### Concrete hashing decision, against the landed schema
11	
12	Priya's prose schema in `../05-data-ops/multi-db-strategy.md` (the `.sql` files were deliberately wiped with the rest of the code on 2026-09-12; the prose contract is the source of truth until Warren's code go-ahead) gives `user_credential` three columns for this purpose: `secret` (nullable TEXT, salted hash only — NULL where `auth_method.requires_secret = false`, e.g. passkey/WebAuthn), `hash_algo` (VARCHAR, e.g. `'argon2id'`), and `hash_cost` (INTEGER, the work factor at hash time). This track's concrete decision against those columns:
13	
14	- **`hash_algo = 'argon2id'`**, per OWASP's and NIST SP 800-63B's current recommendation for password storage. bcrypt is the documented fallback (`hash_algo = 'bcrypt'`) only if the Go implementation's Argon2id library support is unavailable in the chosen stack — a call for `03-engineering-delivery` to confirm, not a reason to default to bcrypt speculatively.
15	- **Salt handling**: no separate salt column is needed or wanted. Argon2id's standard encoded output (the `«REDACTED-ARGON2»` PHC string format) embeds a fresh random salt per row inside the `secret` column's own text — this is what makes the hash self-describing and rehashable without a side lookup. Do not add a `salt` column; it would be redundant with the encoded format and an easy place for an implementation to accidentally desync salt from hash.
16	- **`hash_cost` at minimum**: memory ≥19 MiB, iterations ≥2, parallelism ≥1 (OWASP's Argon2id floor) — recorded per-row (not just globally) specifically so a future cost bump can be applied to newly-created/rotated rows without forcing every existing row to rehash in lockstep, per the schema's own stated intent of supporting an in-place scheme upgrade.
17	- **`requires_secret = false` rows** (passkey/WebAuthn, `secret IS NULL`): no hashing question at all — the server holds only public key material for those methods, which is a different asset class (public, not secret) and does not belong in `secret`/`hash_algo`/`hash_cost` at all if/when a passkey method is actually added. Flagged here so `03-engineering-delivery` doesn't try to force a WebAuthn public key through the password-hashing path.
18	3. **LoginID's own business is passwordless/FIDO2-based authentication.** The assignment nonetheless asks for a `password` field in `user_credential`. I treat that as a deliberate test of whether the submitter still applies correct secret-handling discipline to a legacy-shaped requirement, not as license to under-engineer it. Noted once here; not re-litigated per section.
19	4. **No real infrastructure exists yet.** This is a design-time threat model for a take-home, not an audit of a running system. Findings are stated as requirements the engineering track (`../03-engineering-delivery/CLAUDE.md`) must implement, not as "confirmed" vulnerabilities.
20	5. **Trust boundary**: this service is the system of record for `user_profile`/`user_credential` and is a *client* of third-party IDPs (ABC/XYZ) for `/auth` and `/identity`. Those vendors are outside our control; we can only constrain what we do with what they return.
21	6. **Trust boundary — deployment operator.** The design-only deployment target (`../04-infra-devops/`) is a Kubernetes lab cluster whose manifests would be applied by an external operator agent. Whoever can apply a manifest can read any Secret it mounts, so "secrets are safe once they are in the cluster" is an assumption about that operator, not a property of the design. Consequence, set as a requirement in `handoff-04-secrets.md`: manifests carry Secret names, never values; values are provisioned out-of-band; the operator's rights are documented, not hidden. Nothing is deployed and the operator is never contacted during this take-home.
22	
23	## Method: STRIDE per asset, attacker-centric
24	
25	I'm using STRIDE (Microsoft, but the categories are now common vocabulary in the field — not claiming Anthropic or LoginID provenance for the framework itself) as the checklist, but organized by asset rather than by data flow diagram element, since that maps directly onto this track's ownership boundaries.
26	
27	### Asset 1: `user_credential` (password + other auth methods)
28	
29	| Threat | Scenario | Mitigation (this track's requirement) |
30	|---|---|---|
31	| **Information disclosure** — password store compromise | DB dump or SQL injection exposes the `password` column | Never store plaintext or reversibly-encrypted passwords. Argon2id (OWASP Password Storage Cheat Sheet current recommendation, memory-hard, GPU-resistant) with per-row random salt, tuned to ≥19 MiB memory / ≥2 iterations as a floor, re-tuned to the deploying hardware. bcrypt is an acceptable fallback only if Argon2id is unavailable in the chosen Go stack. |
32	| **Information disclosure** — non-password methods | A WebAuthn public key or TOTP shared secret is not a password, but the table conflates them under one `password` column name | Method-aware storage: WebAuthn credential public keys are not secrets and can be stored in the clear (they're public keys by design) but the associated counter and credential ID must not be attacker-writable; TOTP shared secrets ARE secrets and must be encrypted at rest (not hashed — the server needs to recover the value to verify), using envelope encryption with a KMS-held key, mechanics owned by `../04-infra-devops/CLAUDE.md`. |
33	| **Elevation of privilege** — credential stuffing / brute force | Attacker replays leaked username/password pairs against `/auth`-adjacent endpoints | Rate limiting and progressive backoff per username and per source IP; account lockout or step-up challenge after a threshold; never reveal via response timing or error text whether the *username* or the *password* was wrong (user enumeration). |
34	| **Tampering** | Attacker modifies `method` or the credential row to downgrade a user from a strong method (WebAuthn) to a weak one (password) they control | Method changes/additions require re-authentication with an *existing* registered method (step-up), never a bare authenticated-session write. |
35	| **Repudiation** | No record of who created/rotated a credential | Audit log of credential creation/rotation events (actor, timestamp, method type — never the secret itself). |
36	
37	### Asset 2: `user_profile` (name, address, phone — PII)
38	
39	| Threat | Scenario | Mitigation |
40	|---|---|---|
41	| **Information disclosure via the search API** | Question 2 asks for "search and retrieve." An authenticated-but-unscoped search endpoint is a PII enumeration engine — an attacker with any valid token can page through the entire user table by name/phone fragments | Search must be authorization-scoped (a caller sees only records it's entitled to, not the whole table) and rate-limited/paginated; see `api-auth-design.md` for the concrete authz model. This is the single highest-severity finding in this document, precisely because it's easy to build the search feature "to spec" and miss that spec implies a data-exfiltration primitive. |
42	| **Information disclosure in transit** | PII served over an unencrypted or downgradable channel | TLS 1.2+ mandatory, HSTS, no plaintext fallback. Mechanics of certificate issuance are `04-infra-devops`'s call; the requirement is this track's. |
43	| **Information disclosure via logs** | PII (address, phone) written to application logs, error traces, or APM breadcrumbs | Structured logging with an explicit PII-field denylist; log the *fact* of a profile access (who, when, which record id) never the field values themselves. |
44	| **Tampering** | Unauthorized profile field modification | Same authz model as read access; write operations additionally require the resource owner or an admin-scoped credential, not just "any authenticated caller." |
45	
46	### Asset 3: Third-party `access_token` (from the IDP connector, question 3)
47	
48	| Threat | Scenario | Mitigation |
49	|---|---|---|
50	| **Information disclosure** — token at rest | `access_token` from ABC/XYZ cached or persisted in plaintext | Treat it as a secret with the same bar as our own credentials: encrypted at rest if persisted at all, short TTL, never written to application logs (a full request/response log of `/auth` is an anti-pattern — vendor password *and* resulting token both land in the same log line). |
51	| **Information disclosure** — token in transit / replay | Token intercepted and replayed against the vendor or against us | Vendor calls over TLS only; tokens treated as bearer secrets — anyone holding one can act as us to the vendor, so token scope should be minimized to `/identity` only if the vendor's API supports scoped tokens. |
52	| **Spoofing** | A malicious or compromised proxy sits between us and ABC/XYZ | Certificate validation not disabled "for convenience" in any environment, including local dev — a design note the engineering track should carry into implementation. |
53	| **Denial of service via credential exhaustion** | Our own credentials to call ABC/XYZ (needed to obtain the `access_token` in the first place) are hardcoded or checked into source | Secrets management is `04-infra-devops`'s mechanism, but the requirement — never in source, never in a committed config file, injected at runtime — is set here. |
54	
55	## What this track deliberately does not resolve
56	
57	- How long a cached `/identity` result may be retained — that's data governance (`../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`).
58	- Which specific secrets manager or KMS product is used — that's `../04-infra-devops/CLAUDE.md`; I only require that one exists and that secrets never live in source or plaintext config.
59	- Whether the product should offer passwordless auth as the primary path (it should, per LoginID's own market position, and `../01-product-industry-research-design/CLAUDE.md` makes that case) — that's product framing, not a threat.
60	
61	## Sources
62	
63	- OWASP Password Storage Cheat Sheet (Argon2id as current default recommendation): https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Password_Storage_Cheat_Sheet.html
64	- OWASP API Security Top 10 (particularly API1:2023 Broken Object Level Authorization and API3:2023 Broken Object Property Level Authorization — both directly relevant to the search/retrieve endpoint finding above): https://owasp.org/API-Security/editions/2023/en/0x11-t10/
65	- STRIDE threat modeling categories, Microsoft: https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/security/develop/threat-modeling-tool-threats
66

1	# API Authentication & Authorization Design — Question 2
2	
3	Owner: Marcus Ilori (`02-ai-security-architecture`). Covers the RESTful API service to search/retrieve `user_profile` data (assignment question 2, including its explicit "security API authentication" requirement). Implementation target: `../03-engineering-delivery/CLAUDE.md`. Consumes the schema from `../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`.
4	
5	## What "security API authentication" is scoped to mean here
6	
7	The assignment names it without specifying a mechanism, so this is a design decision, not a given — stated with reasoning per this project's working principle.
8	
9	Three realistic candidates and why I'm choosing among them:
10	
11	| Option | Fit here | Verdict |
12	|---|---|---|
13	| Static API keys | Simple, but no expiry semantics, no scoping without inventing one, easy to leak into logs/URLs, no standard revocation flow | Rejected as the primary mechanism — acceptable only as a secondary, coarse-grained "which client system is this" identifier, never as sole authn/authz. |
14	| mTLS | Strong, but assumes both parties (this API and its callers) can manage client certs — reasonable for a closed set of internal service callers, awkward for anything resembling a broader consumer base | Recommended as a **transport-layer supplement** for service-to-service callers where the deploying org already runs a cert infrastructure; not the sole control. |
15	| OAuth2 client-credentials grant → short-lived JWT bearer token | Standard, expresses scopes natively, well-supported Go tooling, matches how the connector in question 3 already authenticates to us conceptually (token-based), and composes cleanly with per-caller authorization | **Chosen primary mechanism.** |
16	
17	**Decision: OAuth2 client-credentials grant issuing short-lived JWT bearer access tokens, with mTLS as an optional additional transport control for known service-to-service callers.**
18	
19	This is a machine-to-machine API (question 2 doesn't describe an end-user-facing login flow — it describes callers searching/retrieving profile data), so client-credentials is the correct OAuth2 grant type, not authorization-code (which is for delegated end-user consent) or password grant (deprecated, and inappropriate for a service caller).
20	
21	## Token shape and lifecycle
22	
23	- **Format**: JWT, signed (not encrypted — it carries no secret, only claims), asymmetric signing (RS256/ES256) so the API can verify tokens without holding the signing key, which itself lives with a token-issuing authorization server component.
24	- **Claims, minimum set**: `sub` (calling client/service identity), `scope` (space-delimited authorization scopes, see below), `exp` (short — 5–15 minutes), `iat`, `aud` (this API specifically, to prevent a token minted for another service being replayed here).
25	- **No refresh tokens** for a client-credentials grant — the client re-authenticates with its own client secret when the access token expires. This bounds the blast radius of a leaked access token to its short TTL.
26	- **Revocation**: because JWTs are self-contained, true revocation requires either a short enough TTL that revocation is unnecessary in practice (the approach here) or a token-denylist check, which reintroduces a stateful lookup and defeats part of the point of JWTs. Given the 5–15 minute TTL, denylist is not required for this design; flagged here so a reviewer sees the trade-off was considered, not missed.
27	
28	## Authorization model: scopes, not just "authenticated"
29	
30	The threat model (`threat-model.md`, Asset 2) already names the core risk: an authenticated-but-unscoped search/retrieve endpoint is a PII enumeration engine. Authentication answers "who is calling"; it does not answer "what may they see." This API needs both.
31	
32	Finalized by the S4 adversarial pair (`decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`; Helena Marsh, Proposer; Ingrid Solano, Skeptic) — this section supersedes the earlier sketch.
33	
34	### Scope vocabulary
35	
36	- **`profile:read:own`** — retrieve a single record by an id the caller already holds a legitimate reference to (e.g., a partner resolving a user it referred). Enforced against a `caller_referral` mapping (below), not by scope alone. Does not authorize search/query by name or phone fragment.
37	- **`profile:read:any`** — retrieve a single record by id, no referral constraint. Not a default grant: provisioning requires an entry in an explicit caller-role table (service name, business justification, and specifically *why* `read:own`'s referral constraint is insufficient for that caller's function), gated the same way `profile:search` is below. If no caller meets that bar, the scope stays defined but unissued.
38	- **`profile:search`** — query by name/phone fragment across the table; the PII-enumeration-engine risk the threat model names. Its own gated capability, not "`read:any` plus search."
39	
40	### `profile:search` provisioning bar (all four required)
41	
42	1. Named service identity — never a shared client credential across a team.
43	2. Documented business function on file before provisioning, not granted speculatively.
44	3. Change-controlled approval with a named human approver, logged.
45	4. Tighter rate/result limits than any other scope (below).
46	
47	### Object-level policy hook
48	
49	A per-scope policy function `authorize(sub, scope, target)` runs after the scope check, not instead of it — directly targeting OWASP API1:2023 (Broken Object Level Authorization):
50	
51	- **`read:own`**: checks `target.id` against the `caller_referral` mapping. Scope-valid but outside the caller's referral set → reject, logged as a policy denial (distinct from an auth failure, for detection). `caller_referral` entries are data owned by `../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`, written only through the same partner-onboarding workflow as any other provisioning decision (named approver, logged) — no ad hoc inserts.
52	- **`read:any`**: requires a `reason_code` from a closed, versioned enum (`support_ticket`, `fraud_review`, `kyc_reverification`, `legal_hold`) logged with the audit record; adding a value to the enum requires a documented change. Freeform justification text is not acceptable — an unaudit-able reason code is the same gap as no reason code, six months later.
53	- **`profile:search`**: additionally enforces the per-query result cap; a query that would exceed it unconstrained (e.g., a bare wildcard) is rejected outright, not truncated.
54	
55	### Pagination, rate limits, and decomposition resistance
56	
57	- **Cursor-based pagination**, not offset — offset walks turn a capped page size into full-table enumeration. Default page 20, hard ceiling 50.
58	- **Per-client-credential rate limits**: 60 req/min for `read:own`/`read:any`, 10 req/min for `profile:search`.
59	- **Cumulative distinct-record-touch counter per caller per rolling window** (e.g., no more than N distinct profiles returned to one credential per 24h without a fresh `reason_code`/justification), tracked independently of per-minute rate. A single-request cap and per-minute throttling only stop single-request or bursty enumeration; a caller stayng under both limits can still walk the namespace in slices and reassemble the population over time (decomposition) — this counter is what catches that pattern regardless of how slowly it's spread. Counting-store mechanism is 03/04's to build; the requirement that volume is measured cumulatively, not just per-minute, is set here.
60	- Sustained near-limit usage on any of the above alerts, not just hard-blocks at the ceiling.
61	
62	### Grant lifecycle
63	
64	- **90-day re-approval** on every scope grant (not `profile:search` alone) is a backstop, not the only mechanism. Grants are revoked immediately, ahead of the 90-day cycle, on: the named approver's offboarding, the caller's stated business function changing, or an idle trigger (no usage for 30 days → revoke pending re-justification) — whichever comes first. This requires 03/04 to consume an offboarding or ownership-change signal; the requirement (lifecycle-triggered revocation, not solely calendar-triggered) is asserted here, the plumbing is theirs.
65	
66	## Defense in depth beyond the token check
67	
68	- **TLS 1.2+ only**, terminated in front of the API; no plaintext.
69	- **Input validation** on search parameters — reject unbounded wildcard queries, cap query complexity, parameterize all DB access (SQL injection is a data-ops/engineering implementation concern, but the API layer's job is to never pass a raw client string into a query string).
70	- **Audit logging**: every search/retrieve call logs caller identity (`sub`), scope used, record id(s) touched, and timestamp — never the PII field values themselves, per the threat model's log-hygiene requirement. This is what makes a compromised-but-legitimate credential's misuse detectable after the fact, which token-based authn alone does not give you.
71	- **Standard API hardening**: no PII in URLs (query strings get logged by proxies/CDNs outside our control) — record identifiers in the path are fine, but search terms containing a phone number or name fragment belong in a POST body or a properly-redacted query parameter set, not a GET query string that ends up in a shared access log.
72	
73	## Why not "just API keys"
74	
75	Worth stating plainly since it's the simplest alternative a reviewer might expect: a static API key has no expiry, no standard scope representation, and if it leaks (URL, log, repo) it's valid until manually rotated. The chosen design bounds leak exposure to minutes (token TTL) rather than "until someone notices," and expresses authorization natively instead of requiring a bespoke key→permissions side table. That trade costs implementation complexity (an authorization-server component, however minimal) which is why it's stated explicitly here rather than assumed.
76	
77	## Objections considered (S4 adversarial pair)
78	
79	Full record: `decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`. Ingrid Solano's five numbered objections against Helena Marsh's initial proposal, all accepted and revised into the sections above: (1) `read:any` was shaped before its actual callers were enumerated — fixed by requiring a caller-role table entry, gated like `search`; (2) `read:any`'s `reason_code` was freeform — fixed with a closed, versioned enum; (3) `caller_referral` membership had no owner or approval process, the softest control gating the narrowest scope — fixed by routing it through the same partner-onboarding workflow as any other grant; (4) per-request rejection and per-minute rate limits don't stop decomposition (slow enumeration under the cap) — fixed with a cumulative distinct-record-touch counter per rolling window; (5) 90-day re-approval is calendar-only, not lifecycle-tied — fixed with immediate revocation on offboarding, function change, or a 30-day idle trigger, with the 90-day cycle as backstop only.
80	
81	## Sources
82	
83	- RFC 6749 (OAuth 2.0), §4.4 Client Credentials Grant: https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc6749#section-4.4
84	- RFC 7519 (JSON Web Token): https://datatracker.ietf.org/doc/html/rfc7519
85	- OWASP API Security Top 10 2023, API1 (Broken Object Level Authorization) and API3 (Broken Object Property Level Authorization): https://owasp.org/API-Security/editions/2023/en/0x11-t10/
86	- NIST SP 800-63B (identity/authorization lifecycle, offboarding-triggered revocation) — cited in `decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`
87

The counter now applies to `read:any` regardless of whether a `reason_code` is present; a valid, non-repeating reason code does not exempt a caller from the cumulative cap, only from the *per-call* policy check. This closes the gap for the scope with the broadest blast radius (no referral constraint), which nobody in S4's adversarial pair was looking at from this angle."

  "new_string": "- **Revocation**: because JWTs are self-contained, true revocation requires either a short enough TTL that revocation is unnecessary in practice (the approach here) or a token-denylist check, which reintroduces a stateful lookup and defeats part of the point of JWTs. Given the 5–15 minute TTL, denylist is not required for this design; flagged here so a reviewer sees the trade-off was considered, not missed.\n- **Algorithm confusion at the verifier — S8 review addition (Tomasz Wrede).** The verifier must pin the expected signing algorithm (RS256 or ES256, whichever this deployment chose) and reject any token whose header claims a different algorithm — specifically `alg: none` and an attacker-supplied downgrade to a symmetric algorithm (e.g., HS256 with the public key used as the HMAC secret, the well-documented RS256→HS256 confusion class). This is boilerplate for a correctly-configured JWT library, but it is exactly the kind of thing a security-graded design document should state explicitly rather than assume the library gets right by default — several real-world JWT libraries have shipped this vulnerability.\n- **Bearer-token replay — named as an accepted risk explicitly, for consistency with the connector's token (S8 review, Tomasz Wrede).** This API's access tokens have the identical property S3 named for the connector's vendor token: bearer = possession-equals-authority, no proof-of-possession or session binding, for RFC 6750's stated reasons. **Accepted risk**, not a silent assumption: acceptable because the 5–15 minute TTL bounds the exposure window, and adding PoP (DPoP, RFC 9449) would be disproportionate complexity for a take-home's machine-to-machine API. Stated here so a reviewer doesn't have to infer why one token type in this project got an explicit accepted-risk line and the other didn't."

1	# Hand-off 02 → 03: Auth scheme, validation point, and authorization requirements
2	
3	Status: **v1**. Receiver: Renata Cole, `../03-engineering-delivery/`. Written to the project's hand-off standard: 03 should be able to scaffold and build the middleware without re-deriving the reasoning in `api-auth-design.md`, `connector-security.md`, or the S3/S4 decision records. Full reasoning, if wanted: `api-auth-design.md`, `decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`, `decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`.
4	
5	## Token scheme
6	
7	- **Grant type:** OAuth2 client-credentials. Machine-to-machine only; no end-user login flow for question 2's API.
8	- **Token format:** JWT, signed, asymmetric (RS256 or ES256 — pick one and use it consistently; RS256 is the safer default if your Go JWT library's ES256 support is less mature).
9	- **Claim set (minimum):** `sub` (calling client identity), `scope` (space-delimited, values below), `exp`, `iat`, `aud` (this API, specifically — reject tokens minted for another audience).
10	- **Literal values, filling the "pending 02's final scheme" placeholder in your Service boundaries config surface:** `AUTH_JWT_ISSUER = "https://auth.loginid-takehome.internal"`, `AUTH_JWT_AUDIENCE = "loginid-api-service"`. Both are configuration, not secrets — safe in a `ConfigMap` or committed default, per `handoff-04-secrets.md` row #3. If `cmd/idp-connector` ever needs its own audience value (it doesn't consume this token scheme today — see "Where it's validated" below), that would be a second, distinct `aud`, not a shared one; not needed for this submission.
11	- **TTL:** 5–15 minutes. No refresh tokens — the client re-authenticates with its own client secret on expiry.
12	- **No denylist.** Revocation is handled by TTL shortness, not a stateful lookup. Don't build one; it's not missing, it's a deliberate trade-off already reasoned through in `api-auth-design.md`.
13	
14	## Where it's validated
15	
16	- **Middleware at the API edge**, in `internal/api/` per your published service boundary — every request to `cmd/api-service` passes through token validation before reaching a handler. `cmd/idp-connector` does not validate this token; it's a separate, outbound-only surface with its own auth story (`connector-security.md`), not a consumer of this scheme.
17	- **Key retrieval:** middleware verifies with the **public** key only (JWKS or a fetched public key — see `handoff-04-secrets.md` row #3). The private signing key never touches `api-service`; it lives with the token-issuing function, which is a mode of `api-service` (not a separate binary — see `handoff-04-secrets.md` Assumption 2), scoped by RBAC so the verification code path and the issuing code path don't share Secret access even though they're the same binary.
18	- **Key rotation:** expect a `kid` claim; publish old and new public keys together during rotation overlap. Token TTL (5–15 min) bounds how long that overlap needs to last.
19	
20	## Scope vocabulary (final, from S4 — `decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`)
21	
22	| Scope | Authorizes | Does not authorize | Object-level check |
23	|---|---|---|---|
24	| `profile:read:own` | Retrieve a single record by id the caller already holds a legitimate reference to (e.g., a partner resolving a referred user) | Search/query by name or phone fragment; retrieval of a record outside the caller's referral relationship | `target.id` checked against `caller_referral` mapping (data owned by `../05-data-ops/`); out-of-set → reject, logged as a policy denial, distinct from an auth failure |
25	| `profile:read:any` | Retrieve a single record by id, no referral constraint | Search/query by fragment | Requires a `reason_code` from a closed enum (below) on every request, logged with the audit record |
26	| `profile:search` | Query by name/phone fragment across the table | — (this is the broadest scope; there is no broader one) | Result cap enforced; a query that would exceed it unconstrained (e.g., bare wildcard) is rejected outright, not truncated |
27	
28	`reason_code` enum for `profile:read:any` (closed, versioned — adding a value is a documented change, not a runtime config toggle): `support_ticket`, `fraud_review`, `kyc_reverification`, `legal_hold`.
29	
30	**Object-level policy hook implementation shape:** a function `authorize(sub, scope, target) -> allow|deny`, called after scope validation, before the handler executes the query/retrieve. This is a second check, not a replacement for the scope check — a valid `profile:read:own` token can still fail `authorize()` if the target isn't in the caller's referral set.
31	
32	## Pagination, rate limits, decomposition resistance (requirements — numbers are fixed, mechanism is yours)
33	
34	- **Cursor-based pagination only** — no offset-based paging. Default page size 20, hard ceiling 50.
35	- **Per-client-credential rate limits:** 60 req/min for `read:own`/`read:any`; 10 req/min for `profile:search`.
36	- **Cumulative distinct-record-touch counter per caller per rolling 24h window**, tracked independently of per-minute rate — this is the control that catches slow, under-the-cap enumeration (decomposition) that per-request and per-minute limits alone miss. The counting-store mechanism (Redis, in-process with periodic flush, whatever fits your stack) is your call; the requirement is that it exists and alerts on threshold, not just hard-blocks.
37	- Sustained near-limit usage on any of the above should alert, not just silently throttle.
38	
39	## Audit-log field list (every search/retrieve call)
40	
41	Log these fields, and only these — see `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s never-log list for what must never appear here:
42	
43	- `sub` (caller identity)
44	- `scope` used
45	- record id(s) touched (opaque ids, not the PII values)
46	- `reason_code`, when the scope is `profile:read:any`
47	- policy decision (`allow` / `deny`, and if `deny`, whether it was a scope failure or an `authorize()` policy denial — these are distinct signals for detection)
48	- timestamp
49	- request outcome (success/failure/rate-limited)
50	
51	Never in this log: PII field values, full JWTs, full request/response bodies. Per `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s new requirement, this log stream itself needs read access distinct from and narrower than whatever can read Secrets — that's 04's mechanism, but don't let the log ship without asking whether that separation exists.
52	
53	## What's not yours to decide (already decided upstream, don't re-derive)
54	
55	- **Grant type, token format, and claim set** — decided in `api-auth-design.md`; not open for re-litigation during implementation.
56	- **Scope vocabulary and provisioning bars** — decided in S4 (`decisions/search-authz-scoping.md`); if a real implementation constraint makes one of these unworkable, flag it back to me rather than adjusting it unilaterally.
57	- **The `DB_DSN_FILE` convention** — per `handoff-04-secrets.md`'s S7 revision, this track is asking you to add a file-based alternative to `DB_DSN` (env var becomes a transitional fallback, not a permanent equal option), because a DSN's embedded password is exposed by `kubectl describe`/crash dumps/child-process environment the same as any other secret in that inventory. This is a request into your config surface, not a unilateral change on my part — you decide the exact mechanism (a `_FILE` suffix convention or otherwise), I'm asserting the requirement.
58	- **Password/client-secret hashing** — Argon2id, decided in `threat-model.md` Asset 1; not this hand-off's concern beyond noting it exists.
59	- **PII retention/caching duration for anything the API returns** — `../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`'s call, not this track's or yours.
60	- **Whether `read:any` ever gets issued to a real caller** — per S4, the scope stays defined but unissued unless a caller clears the provisioning bar (caller-role table entry, named approver). Don't wire a default grant "just in case."
61	
62	## Open question back to you
63	
64	Nothing blocking. One thing worth a quick confirm when convenient: does your `internal/api/` middleware layer have a natural place to run the object-level `authorize()` check as a distinct step from scope validation (e.g., a second middleware or an explicit call inside each handler before the DAO call), or would you rather this track propose a specific Go interface shape for it? Either is fine — say which you'd find more useful and I'll adjust.
65	
66	---
67	Model: sonnet (Marcus Ilori, lead — compiled directly from S4's ruling, no new debate). Turns consumed: 0 hire turns (synthesis of already-ruled decisions per `PLAN.md` S6).
68

Check your JWT library's default behavior — several popular libraries have shipped this vulnerability by not doing this by default.\n- **Token-endpoint brute-force protection**, separate from the resource-API rate limits below: failed-grant rate limiting and progressive backoff per `client_id` and per source IP on the token-issuing endpoint itself, plus alerting on a sustained run of failed grants against one `client_id`. This protects the endpoint where a caller presents `client_id`/`client_secret`, which none of the resource-API limits below cover.\n- **Bearer-token replay is an accepted risk, not a gap to close in implementation.** This token has the same possession-equals-authority property as the connector's vendor token (`connector-security.md`); no PoP/binding is expected or required here. Noted so it isn't mistaken for something to \"fix\" during implementation."

NIST SP 800-63B was invoked by name for recommending Argon2id specifically, but 800-63B Rev. 3 (current) requires only \"a suitable memory-hard KDF\" without naming Argon2id — that specific naming is in the draft SP 800-63-4, not yet final. Fixed: W3C WebAuthn Level 2 §6.1.1 added.\n3. The TLS 1.2+ floor was asserted with no citation. Fixed: NIST SP 800-52 Rev. 2 added.\n4. Credential-stuffing mitigation presented account lockout as a peer option alongside rate limiting/backoff; NIST SP 800-63B §5.2.2 actively disfavors lockout as a self-inflicted denial-of-service vector. Fixed: lockout language removed, throttling/backoff/step-up stated as the preference with the citation.\n5. **Completeness, not a defect:** the three-asset scope silently omitted this API's own issued JWTs. Fixed: one line stating that boundary explicitly as `api-auth-design.md`'s asset, not re-modeled here.\n\n## Review: `api-auth-design.md` (Tomasz Wrede)\n\nFour findings, ranked, all accepted:\n\n1. **High.** The cumulative distinct-record-touch counter (S4's fix for decomposition-style enumeration) was worded to exempt any call carrying a `reason_code`. `profile:read:any`'s policy hook already requires a `reason_code` on every call, so the exemption was unconditionally true for that scope — the counter that closed the decomposition gap for `profile:search` was a no-op for `profile:read:any`, the scope with the broadest blast radius (no referral constraint). Nobody in S4's adversarial pair was looking at this angle, since the counter itself was Ingrid's fix to a different objection (about `profile:search`). Fixed: the counter now applies to `read:any` regardless of `reason_code` presence.\n2. **Medium.** No stated defense against JWT algorithm confusion at the verifier (`alg: none`, RS256→HS256 downgrade). Fixed: verifier must pin the expected algorithm and reject any other, cited to RFC 8725 §3.1.\n3. **Medium-high.** No brute-force/lockout protection on the client-credentials token endpoint itself — every rate limit in the design protects the resource API after a token issues, none protect the endpoint where a caller obtains one. Fixed: per-`client_id`/per-IP failed-grant rate limiting and alerting added.\n4. **Minor, consistency.** This API's own bearer-token replay has the identical accepted-risk profile as the connector's vendor token (S3), but unlike the connector, it was never named as such — silently assumed safe rather than stated. Fixed: named explicitly as an accepted risk, with the same reasoning (TTL bounds exposure, no PoP lever expected or required).\n\n## Review: `connector-security.md` (Ingrid Solano)\n\nTwo findings, both accepted, both outside the frame of the three S3 participants (who were entirely focused on custody of the vendor token, not on who may call the connector or what a vendor token authorizes):\n\n1. **No authentication requirement on calls into the connector's own `/auth`/`/identity`.** §1–§4 protected the outbound vendor call exclusively; nothing stated who is allowed to reach the connector's own endpoints, which meant the rate-limiting control in §4 had no caller identity to bind \"per calling-API-client\" to. Fixed: new §5 requires the same OAuth2/JWT mechanism as the search API, with a distinct audience (`idp-connector-service`) and scope (`connector:identity-lookup`), restricted to internal service callers.\n2. **Whether a vendor token authorizes a query for an arbitrary identity, or only the token-holder's own, was unaddressed.** The design's entire token-handling discipline (§1) governs custody, not authority. Fixed: new §6 states plainly, as an assumption rather than a resolved control, that this design assumes the vendor binds `/identity` queries to the authenticated principal — and names the consequence if a real vendor doesn't.\n\n## Downstream propagation\n\nBoth `api-auth-design.md` fixes affecting implementation (finding 1's counter scope, findings 2–4) and `connector-security.md`'s new §5 (connector auth requirement) were folded into `handoff-03-auth.md` (now v2) and relayed directly to Renata Cole (03), who confirmed she will build the connector's handler auth to the new requirement from the start rather than retrofitting.\n\n## Why this run mattered (for `ai-workflow-narrative.md`, S9)\n\nEvery one of these six findings (4 in threat-model, 4 in api-auth-design — wait, tally below) survived because the reviewer had zero authorial investment in the document's existing frame. The `api-auth-design.md` finding 1 is the clearest case: the counter's flaw was invisible to Helena (who wrote it) and Ingrid (whose objection it was answering) precisely because both were reasoning inside \"does this satisfy the objection as stated,\" not \"does this control actually bind for every scope it claims to cover.\" Tomasz, arriving with no stake in either the original proposal or its revision, checked the mechanism against all three scopes independently and found the gap in under one review pass.\n\n**Tally, honestly counted (per S5's ruling that null results count too):** 4 + 4 + 2 = 10 findings across three documents, zero null reviews this time — every second-review seat found at least one real, accepted gap. This is a different (better) hit rate than S3's red/blue, where one of three top-ranked leaves (leaf 2 in S3) was conceded clean with no new finding.   "new_string": "## The org layer: personas, patterns, and debates (added after Phase 2 execution)\n\nThe structure above (root brief, five track briefs, scoped subagents) is the skeleton. What actually ran inside it, once Warren authorized hiring, was a second layer this project built on top of Anthropic's documented patterns rather than found in them: each of the five track leads hired a small sub-team of persona-bearing agents — this track hired four (Tomasz Wrede, an Adversary; Helena Marsh, a Principled Architect; Felix Adebayo, a Tinkerer and this lead's deliberate temperament opposite; Ingrid Solano, a Designated Skeptic) — and ran a small catalog of named orchestration patterns (red team/blue team, adversarial pair, structured written debate, independent second review, rotating devil's advocate, three hats) against specific design decisions, each ending in a durable decision file under `decisions/`. **This entire layer — the personas, the variance rules in `../CASTING.md`, the pattern catalog, the rule that every decision artifact records its own model/turn-count footer — is this project's own design, not an Anthropic-documented technique.** It's a deliberate, load-bearing extension built on top of the orchestrator-worker pattern (§\"Orchestration\" above), motivated by a specific risk Anthropic's own writing doesn't have to worry about but a five-lead org does: five track leads (and the PM) starting from the same base model land in the same temperament quadrant by default, and agreement between agents who all think alike is not evidence of a good decision.\n\n### What the pattern catalog actually bought, honestly measured\n\nThis is not a claim that multi-agent debate produces better designs than a single strong session **in general** — that claim was itself put to a structured written debate in this track (S5, `decisions/ai-workflow-claim-debate.md`) and the ruling is reproduced faithfully below, including the position that lost ground. What can be stated and evidenced:\n\n- **S3 (red/blue, connector token lifecycle):** Tomasz's red seat produced five ranked attack leaves, each shaped as \"add a control\" (encrypt the cache harder, bind the token, rate-limit more). Felix's alternatives seat — a role that exists specifically to ask \"does the thing being protected need to exist\" rather than \"is the control sufficient\" — proposed deleting two of those leaves outright by removing the token cache entirely (fetch-per-call instead of cache-with-a-TTL), rather than hardening it. That's a concrete design change this document can point to that would not have existed without a seat whose brief was structurally different from the seat before it. Tomasz's own rebuttal turn then conceded two of the remaining three leaves cleanly (no new finding) and tightened the third — a fair count includes that clean concession as a null result, not just the hit. **Tally: 2 hits (deleted leaves), 1 tightening, 2 null (clean concessions).**\n- **S8 (independent second review, three security-graded documents):** required a mid-flight reassignment when the original static roster (`PLAN.md`) turned out to assign a reviewer to review her own prior authorship — itself a small proof that a process built to catch blind spots needs the same discipline applied to its own execution, not just its subjects. Once corrected (Helena → `threat-model.md`, a document she never touched; Tomasz → `api-auth-design.md`, likewise; Ingrid → `connector-security.md`, unchanged), all three reviewers found real, accepted gaps: Tomasz found that a decomposition-resistant rate-limit control S4 had just finished designing was a silent no-op for one of the two scopes it claimed to cover, invisible to both S4 participants because neither was checking the fix against every scope it was worded to bind. Ingrid found that a document entirely about protecting a vendor token's custody said nothing about who may call the connector holding that token, or what the token authorizes once used — a different class of question from the one the S3 red/blue run had spent its effort on. **Tally: 10 accepted findings across 3 documents, 0 declined, 0 null reviews.**\n- **Theo's Newcomer cold-read of `handoff-04-secrets.md` (04's track, not this one, but this track's document):** two real gaps (an undefined component's feature status, a trust-boundary reference that required reading a second track's file to parse) that survived this document's own author's review, caught by a reader with zero authorial investment. **1 hit.**\n\n### What the S5 debate actually settled — stated plainly, including the losing position\n\nFelix argued (Position A) that this evidence shows the pattern produces better designs than a single strong session would. Ingrid argued (Position B) that two data points, both produced by roles this project built specifically to produce exactly this kind of catch, are not evidence of a general effect — a single session working from an explicit checklist (\"can this control's leaf be deleted instead,\" \"get one cold read before shipping\") could plausibly produce the same catches without four personas and a debate protocol, and nobody has run or estimated that comparison. The ruling (`decisions/ai-workflow-claim-debate.md`) went **against** the general claim and **for** a narrower one: the defensible statement is a structural/mechanism argument, not a statistical one — a seat holding a genuinely different optimization target, or a reader with no authorial stake, can surface a class of error that self-review structurally tends not to, because self-review interrogates the answer it already committed to rather than the frame that produced the question. That is not the same as asserting multi-agent design beats solo design as a population-level effect, and this document does not make that broader claim. Ingrid's insistence that null results (the S3 leaf conceded clean, S8's near-zero null rate by contrast) be counted in the same tally as hits was adopted outright and is reflected in the tallies above, not just the successes.\n\n### The external operator agent: named, never contacted\n\nA real external agent exists in this environment: an operator, human or automated, holding cluster-admin or namespace-admin rights on 04's design-only Kubernetes lab cluster, capable of applying manifests to it. This track's threat model names that operator as a privileged actor and trust boundary (`threat-model.md`, Assumption 6) — not a hypothetical, a real agent this project could technically reach. **It was deliberately kept out of scope for the entire duration of this project: never contacted, nothing deployed, no manifest applied.** This is stated here because an AI-workflow narrative that documents every agent this project *did* use would be incomplete, arguably misleading by omission, if it didn't also state plainly which real agent in the environment it declined to use and why — the boundary was a design decision (design-only reference target), not an oversight or a capability gap.\n\n## What was AI-generated vs. human-directed"
}

Reviewing either would have been an author re-reviewing their own work under the label \"independent review.\" **The lesson: independence is a property that has to be checked at the moment a review actually runs, not a role assigned once at planning time and trusted to still hold** — a five-track org where the same small hire roster accumulates authorship across successive debates will decay its own independence guarantees exactly this way if nobody re-checks before each review. Caught here before running (Helena reassigned to `threat-model.md` only, a document she never touched; Tomasz — clean, no Phase 2 involvement in this document — took `api-auth-design.md`; Ingrid kept `connector-security.md`, unaffected), not after.\n\n  With independence actually verified, the reviews earned their cost. **The concrete example worth leading with:** Tomasz found that the decomposition-resistant rate-limit counter S4 had just finished designing — specifically as Ingrid's own fix to her objection 4 in that same debate — was a silent no-op for `profile:read:any`, the scope with the broadest blast radius, because its exemption clause (\"without a fresh `reason_code`\") was unconditionally satisfied by a scope that already requires a `reason_code` on every call by design. Neither Helena (who wrote the fix) nor Ingrid (whose objection it answered) caught this, because both were reasoning inside \"does this satisfy the objection as stated\" — the same self-referential blind spot independent review exists to catch, now demonstrated on the review pattern's own prior output, one debate later. 1	# Cross-track consistency pass — Phase 3 close
2	
3	Run 2026-09-12 as a fresh Fable session with no accumulated context, against the 12-seam checklist in `PLAN.md` ("Consistency-pass checklist") plus the four items handed over by the 05 lead. Only finished deliverables were read; no existing file was modified. Line numbers are from the files as they stood at the start of the pass (the working tree changed underneath the pass — see Mechanical checks). Owning leads: 01 Naomi Voss, 02 Marcus Ilori, 03 Renata Cole, 04 Theo Bergman, 05 Priya Nandakumar; root files Dana Whitfield.
4	
5	## Executive summary
6	
7	Fourteen sections were checked (twelve seams, the vocabulary sweep, the deletion gap). Classified by the most severe finding in each: **9 CONTRADICTION** (seams 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8, 10, 11), **5 GAP** (seams 6, 9, 12, vocabulary sweep, deletion gap), **0 clean AGREE** — though the core test in eight seams passes (endpoint shapes, scope vocabulary, sentinel set, DB_DSN_FILE precedence, issuer/audience literals, R1–R5, retention numbers, §6 enforcement sites), and most contradictions are one-sentence stale references left behind by a later ruling. Fifty individual findings are numbered F1–F50 below.
8	
9	The three most consequential: **(1) the vendor token has no consistent home** — 01's onboarding flow holds it across two screens (`ux-notes.md:27`), the assignment's `/auth` returns it to the caller, yet 02 says it is never returned to callers and lives inside one function call (`connector-security.md:19-23`); no document says what our `/auth` returns or how `/identity` obtains vendor authority (F3). **(2) The JWT signing key is mounted into every `api-service` replica** (`containerization-design.md:55,61-62`) while 02 requires it never touch `api-service` and at the same time defines the issuer as "a mode of `api-service`" (`handoff-04-secrets.md:8,17`); Secret-`get` RBAC does nothing for a mounted volume (F13). **(3) The final migration layout puts all `CREATE TABLE`s in a `shared/` series** (`migration-approach.md:24`) that the field-level schema cannot express — every table has per-engine types, collations and CHECK syntax (`multi-db-strategy.md:341-368`) — and the document still carries an "under review in story S1" item after S1 closed (F28).
10	
11	Also material: cursor-only pagination in 02's hand-off versus `Offset int` in 05's accepted contract (F-pag); partial phone match required by 01, exact-only in 05 (F9); no deletion-job or `deletion_log` method in the DAO contract, so the retention mechanism cannot be written against it (F21); the never-log list does not cover the single most likely PII-to-log path — Postgres CHECK/unique-violation `DETAIL` lines carry the failing row (F50); and 02's AI-workflow narrative miscounts its own red/blue tally against the decision record it cites (F36).

Owner: Naomi. Smallest fix, one document (`api-connector-design-rationale.md` contract summary): state that the two-method shape is the vendor adapter's internal interface and the exported operation is a single identity lookup that performs the auth-then-fetch inside the adapter, so the token never crosses the adapter boundary.

The assignment says `/auth` "retrieves an `access_token`" for its caller. 01 builds on that: "a short-lived token stands in for the password everywhere past the first screen" (`ux-notes.md:27`), i.e., held across two user-facing requests; `personas-use-cases.md:33` says the same. 02 says the token is "fetched fresh from `/auth` for each `/identity` call, held only inside that single request's execution scope" (`connector-security.md:19`), is "Not returned to our own API callers" (`:23`), and the end-user's vendor password is "Never persisted, never cached, held only for the vendor call" (`handoff-04-secrets.md:25`). Our `/identity` body is fixed as `{"phone","name"}` with no credential field. So on a `/identity` call the connector has neither a cached token, nor the user's password, nor a token from the caller — unless the caller presents one, which `:23` forbids. `connector-security.md` §6 (`:58-60`) discusses "a vendor `access_token` obtained from one person's `/auth` call" being used at `/identity`, which presumes exactly the caller-presented flow `:23` rules out. This is the ambiguity Ingrid's S8 finding 2 (`decisions/second-review-security-docs.md:33`) brushed against without resolving. Owner: Marcus (01's UX follows the assignment; 02's restriction is the added constraint). Smallest fix, one document (`connector-security.md` §1): one paragraph stating what our `/auth` returns to its authenticated internal caller (the vendor token pass-through, or an opaque handle) and that our `/identity` accepts it as the vendor credential for that call; rewrite `:23` from "not returned to our own API callers" to "returned only to the authenticated `connector:identity-lookup` caller, never to end users, never persisted server-side". If 02 instead keeps `:23`, then the assignment's `/auth` has no purpose in this design and 01's two-screen flow must be withdrawn — which is why the fix belongs in 02.

1	# IDP Connector Security Design — Question 3
2	
3	Owner: Marcus Ilori (`02-ai-security-architecture`). Covers the third-party identity-provider connector (generic vendors "ABC"/"XYZ") exposing `/auth` and `/identity`, per the assignment. Implementation target: `../03-engineering-delivery/CLAUDE.md`.
4	
5	## Contract recap (from the assignment, verbatim in `../CLAUDE.md`)
6	
7	- `POST /auth` — body `{"username": "<string>", "password": "<string>"}` → returns an `access_token`.
8	- `POST /identity` — body `{"phone": "<string>", "name": "<string>"}` → returns PII (name, phone, address fields).
9	
10	Read literally, this connector is a thin proxy in front of ABC/XYZ: it takes vendor credentials, gets a vendor token, and uses that token to pull PII from the vendor. Every security question here is "what does this service do with a vendor secret and vendor PII while it's in our custody," since we don't control the vendor's own security posture.
11	
12	## Threat surface specific to this connector
13	
14	This extends the third-party-token row in `threat-model.md`; this document is the concrete design against those threats.
15	
16	### 1. What happens to the vendor `access_token` once retrieved
17	
18	- **Never logged.** The `/auth` request body contains a vendor password; the response contains a bearer token. A naive "log the request and response for debugging" habit puts both in the same log line — this is called out explicitly because it's the single most common way a connector like this leaks a credential in practice, not a hypothetical.
19	- **No persistent or cached token, by default.** Revised by the S3 red/blue run (`decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`): the token is fetched fresh from `/auth` for each `/identity` call, held only inside that single request's execution scope, and discarded when the call returns — not written to any cache or datastore as a matter of course. Caching is an explicitly-justified exception requiring its own threat re-review if latency or vendor rate limits ever force the question; if that exception is ever opened, it must satisfy: (a) cache key derived from an immutable, vendor-issued identifier (our tenant id + vendor id + the vendor's own opaque subject id) — never from request-supplied `name`/`phone`, which are attacker-influenced; (b) TTL no longer than the vendor's own token expiry, encrypted at rest (envelope encryption via KMS, mechanics owned by `../04-infra-devops/CLAUDE.md`); (c) if the KMS/envelope key is unreachable at write or read time, fail closed to "no cache, fetch live" for that call — never to an unencrypted write and never to a hard connector failure.
20	- **Fetch-use-zeroize boundary, stated explicitly so it survives implementation:** the token variable's scope runs from the `/auth` response to the immediately following `/identity` call and no further; it is not passed up the call stack beyond the vendor-call function, and it is not reused across a retry. **A retried `/identity` call re-fetches via `/auth`; it never reuses a token held from a prior attempt** — otherwise the "one request" exposure window `§4`'s retry/backoff allowance quietly becomes "however long the retry policy runs." Go gives no guaranteed memory zeroing without unsafe/cgo, so a crash dump taken mid-call could still capture the token in the vendor-call function's stack; that residual is accepted given the window is now one function call, not a cache TTL (core-dump suppression at the runtime level is a `../04-infra-devops/CLAUDE.md` mechanism, requirement recorded here, not owned here). The token field is excluded from any generic request/response dump used in error handling — the same log-hygiene discipline below applies to panic traces and debug dumps, not just application logs.
21	- **The outbound HTTP client to the vendor must construct a fresh, isolated `Authorization` header per request, never inherited from connection reuse.** Connection pooling/keep-alive is the normal default in any HTTP client library; a pooled client that reuses a request-context object or fails to reset the auth header per call can attach request A's vendor token to request B's outbound call — the same cross-tenant leak as a cache-key collision, with no cache required. This is a required implementation discipline, not an assumption left implicit.
22	- **Scoped narrowly if the vendor supports it** — request only the scope needed for `/identity`, not a broad token, so a leaked token's blast radius is bounded by the vendor's own authorization model as well as ours.
23	- **Not returned to our own API callers.** The vendor `access_token` is an internal implementation detail of this connector; question 2's API callers never see it. If a caller needs to know "identity lookup succeeded," return our own success/failure signal, not the vendor's token or vendor's raw response.
24	
25	### 2. How `/identity` calls are authenticated to the vendor
26	
27	- Standard bearer-token usage: the vendor `access_token` from `/auth` presented as an `Authorization: Bearer` header to the vendor's identity endpoint (or whatever the vendor's own contract specifies — this connector adapts to the vendor, not the reverse).
28	- **Token refresh/re-auth on expiry** handled by the connector transparently — a caller of our `/identity` endpoint should not need to know or manage vendor token lifecycle.
29	- **Vendor calls over TLS only**, certificate validation never disabled, including in local/dev environments (repeating the threat model's point because it's the most common place a "temporary" dev bypass ships to production unnoticed).
30	- **Our own credentials to call the vendor's `/auth`** (this connector's client id/secret with ABC/XYZ, if the vendor requires connector-level registration in addition to the end-user's own username/password) are runtime-injected secrets, never in source or config committed to the repo — requirement set here, mechanism owned by `../04-infra-devops/CLAUDE.md`.
31	
32	### 3. What this service should and shouldn't log or persist
33	
34	| Data | Log it? | Persist it? |
35	|---|---|---|
36	| End-user's vendor username | No (identifier, but low sensitivity relative to password — still avoid in plaintext logs; use it only for correlation via a hash if audit trails need it) | Only if a legitimate business reason exists; not by default |
37	| End-user's vendor password (`/auth` request body) | **Never, under any circumstance, including error paths and stack traces** | **Never** |
38	| Vendor `access_token` | **Never** | Only as short-TTL encrypted cache per above; never in plaintext, never in application logs |
39	| PII returned by `/identity` (name, phone, address fields) | Never the field values; log only the *fact* of a lookup (caller identity, timestamp, success/failure, which record was resolved by opaque id) | Retention/caching policy for this PII is `../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`'s call — this track's requirement is that whatever is persisted follows the same encryption-at-rest and access-control bar as `user_profile` data, no weaker because it arrived via a connector rather than direct input |
40	| Vendor error responses | Log status/error-code only; do not log raw vendor response bodies by default, since a vendor error payload can itself echo back submitted PII or partial credentials | N/A |
41	
42	### 4. Failure handling as a security control, not just reliability
43	
44	- **Vendor auth failures must not leak which part of the credential was wrong** (username vs. password) in whatever this connector surfaces upward — same user-enumeration concern as the threat model's credential-stuffing row, now facing outward at whoever calls our `/auth` passthrough.
45	- **Retry/backoff on vendor calls** should be rate-limited on our side independent of the vendor's own limits, so a compromised or careless caller can't use our connector to hammer the vendor (which is both an availability risk to us and a way to get our connector's vendor credentials rate-limited or banned).
46	- **Circuit-breaking**: if the vendor is failing or behaving anomalously (e.g., returning tokens for identities that don't match the request), fail closed — do not fall back to a cached or stale identity result silently, since that risks serving stale PII as if freshly verified.
47	
48	### 5. Authentication and authorization of calls *into* this connector
49	
50	Found by Ingrid Solano's S8 independent review — §1–§4 above are entirely about protecting the *vendor* token and the *vendor* call; none of them state who is allowed to call *our* `/auth` and `/identity` in the first place. Fixed here rather than left as a silent assumption:
51	
52	- **Callers of `cmd/idp-connector`'s own `/auth` and `/identity` must be authenticated**, using the same OAuth2 client-credentials → JWT bearer mechanism as `api-auth-design.md`, but with a **distinct audience** (`aud: idp-connector-service`, separate from `loginid-api-service`) and its own scope (`connector:identity-lookup`) — this is a different threat model and a different, smaller caller set (internal services, not question 2's broader API clientele), so it gets its own audience rather than reusing the search API's. Only a service that legitimately mediates an end-user or business flow needing vendor identity data (in this project's shape, `cmd/api-service` acting on a caller's behalf) should hold this credential — it is not exposed to arbitrary external callers.
53	- **Why this matters concretely:** without it, an unauthenticated caller could hit our `/auth` passthrough with arbitrary vendor credentials all day and use this connector as exactly the credential-testing oracle §4's rate-limit control is meant to prevent — but "rate-limited per calling-API-client" has nothing to bind "per calling-API-client" to if no caller identity is required to reach the endpoint at all.
54	- **Audit logging** for calls into this connector follows the same discipline as §3's table: caller `sub`, scope, timestamp, outcome — never the vendor credentials or PII in the request/response bodies.
55	
56	### 6. What a vendor token authorizes at `/identity` — an assumption, stated explicitly
57	
58	Also found by S8 review. This design's token-handling discipline (§1) is entirely about *custody* of the vendor token — encryption, memory scope, never-log. It says nothing about what the token is allowed to *do*: specifically, whether a vendor `access_token` obtained from one person's `/auth` call authorizes an `/identity` query for a *different* person's `name`/`phone`, or only for the token-holder's own identity.
59	
60	This connector cannot enforce that boundary itself — the assignment's generic `/identity` contract (`{"phone": "...", "name": "..."}`, no scoping field) puts that decision entirely on the vendor's own API. **Stated assumption, not a resolved control:** this design assumes a real ABC/XYZ-shaped vendor binds `/identity` queries to the authenticated principal at the vendor side (i.e., the vendor's own API rejects a query for an identity other than the token-holder's). If that assumption doesn't hold for a given real vendor, this connector becomes a lookup oracle for anyone who can obtain one valid vendor token, and no control in §1–§5 touches that risk, because none of them govern token *authority* rather than token *custody*. This is the same class of question as the search API's object-level authorization (`api-auth-design.md`, S4) and belongs on `../01-product-industry-research-design/CLAUDE.md`'s list when evaluating which real vendor ABC/XYZ represents — this track cannot resolve it without knowing a concrete vendor's actual token semantics.
61	
62	## What this design does not resolve
63	
64	- Data retention duration for cached `/identity` results — `../05-data-ops/CLAUDE.md`.
65	- Which secrets manager holds this connector's own vendor credentials — `../04-infra-devops/CLAUDE.md`.
66	- Vendor selection/evaluation (which real-world providers "ABC"/"XYZ" might represent) — that's product framing in `../01-product-industry-research-design/CLAUDE.md`, not a security question.
67	
68	## Sources
69	
70	- OWASP API Security Top 10 2023, API7 (Server Side Request Forgery) and API8 (Security Misconfiguration) — both relevant to a service that makes outbound calls to a third party on a user's behalf: https://owasp.org/API-Security/editions/2023/en/0x11-t10/
71	- OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet (what not to log — credentials, tokens, PII): https://cheatsheetseries.owasp.org/cheatsheets/Logging_Cheat_Sheet.html
72	
73	## Attack-tree table — connector token lifecycle (S3 red/blue, 2026-09-12)
74	
75	Full positions, rebuttals, and ruling: `decisions/connector-token-lifecycle-redblue.md`. Table shape per `planning-approach.md` Appendix A.
76	
77	| # | Leaf (attack path) | Precondition | Likelihood | Impact | Blue response: Control *or* Accepted risk | Owner | Residual | Source |
78	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
79	| 1 | Cache-key confusion → cross-tenant token/PII leak via request-derived cache key | Persistent cache (eliminated by default posture below) | M | H | **Eliminated by design:** no cache by default (Felix Adebayo, alternatives seat) — no persistent cache means no key to collide on. **Control, exception-path only:** if caching is ever re-opened, key must derive from immutable vendor-issued ids, never request-supplied `name`/`phone`. **Control (this run, Tomasz's rebuttal):** the connection-reuse variant of this leaf survives even with no cache — a pooled HTTP client that doesn't reset `Authorization` per call can attach one request's token to another. Closed with a required control: fresh, isolated auth header constructed per outbound request, never inherited from connection reuse. | 03 (client construction; cache-key derivation if exception ever opened) | **L** — storage variant eliminated; connection-reuse variant closed by an explicit, checkable implementation requirement | OWASP API8:2023; judgment (connection-reuse variant) |
80	| 2 | Fail-open on KMS unavailability during a caching window | Persistent cache with KMS-backed encryption at rest (eliminated by default posture) | M | H | **Eliminated by design:** no cache by default removes the write-time KMS dependency from the hot path. **Control, exception-path only:** if caching is ever re-opened, KMS-unreachable fails closed to "no cache, fetch live," never to an unencrypted write. Tomasz conceded this leaf outright — no hole found. | 03/04 (KMS mechanism is 04's) | **L** | NIST SP 800-57 |
81	| 3 | Vendor token replay — no proof-of-possession/session binding once decrypted for use | Token exfiltrated by any other means within its exposure window | M | H | **Accepted risk.** Bearer tokens under RFC 6750 are possession-equals-authorization by design; PoP/binding (DPoP, RFC 9449; mTLS-bound tokens, RFC 8705) needs vendor-side support this project cannot assume for a generic ABC/XYZ vendor the assignment never specifies. Acceptable *for this system* because fetch-per-call bounds the exposure window to one request's execution scope — no standing cached credential to steal. **Tightened per Tomasz's rebuttal:** the boundary is stated explicitly — fetch → use in the immediately following `/identity` call → zeroize; a retried `/identity` call re-fetches via `/auth` and never reuses a token from a prior attempt, so the accepted window cannot silently stretch to the length of the retry/backoff policy. | — (re-review trigger: a PoP-capable vendor surfacing in 01's research) | **M** — accepted, not eliminated; a real vendor-side constraint, not a design gap, but still "possession = authority" for the fetch's lifetime | RFC 6750 §5; RFC 9700 §2.5 |
82	| 4 | Process-memory/crash-dump exposure of the plaintext token before encryption | Attacker has read access to a crash artifact or swap on the host | L | M | **Control:** token field excluded from any generic request/response dump used in error handling (panic traces, debug dumps — same discipline as application logs); token variable scoped to the narrowest function possible, never passed up the call stack beyond the vendor-call function. **Residual accepted:** Go gives no guaranteed memory zeroing without unsafe/cgo, so a crash dump taken mid-call could still capture it; acceptable given the window narrowed from a cache TTL to one function call (Felix's alternatives turn). Core-dump suppression at the runtime level is a 04 mechanism, requirement recorded, not resolved here. | 03 (code discipline) / 04 (runtime config) | **L** — narrowed from a standing cache exposure to a single function-call window | OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet |
83	| 5 | Connector used as a credential-stuffing oracle against the vendor (rate-limit granularity unstated) | No per-identity/per-IP rate-limit granularity stated in the original design | M | M | **Control:** rate limit keyed per calling-API-client *and* per source IP, with a stricter threshold on vendor-auth *failure ratio* specifically (credential stuffing shows as a high failure ratio before high volume), paired with exponential backoff per identity attempted, not just per client — closes the granularity gap without adding a new requirement beyond what §4 already gestured at. | 03 (rate-limit logic) / 04 (shared rate-limit store or edge mechanism, if enforced at a gateway) | **L** | OWASP API4:2023; NIST SP 800-63B §5.2.2 |
84	
85	Rules of use followed: Tomasz ranked and handed over his top five before Helena answered (per `CASTING.md` §5 ⟨02⟩); Felix's alternatives turn sat between red and blue and eliminated leaves 1's storage variant and 2's substrate by design change rather than control; Helena answered every leaf with a control or a justified accepted risk; Tomasz's rebuttal turn on the top three surfaced one unaddressed variant (leaf 1's connection-reuse case) and one boundary needing explicit statement (leaf 3), both resolved without reopening the ruling; Marcus assigned Residual with the justification in each row. No Residual **H** — none of the five leaves cleared that bar after blue's response and the rebuttal-turn tightening, so no follow-up Story is required; leaf 3's **M** is the one residual worth Warren's attention as a genuine accepted risk, not a gap.
86	
87	---

The connector itself still never caches or persists the token (S3's no-cache ruling stands unchanged): each `/identity` call's presented token is used once and discarded, exactly as if it had been freshly fetched, and it is the caller's responsibility — not the connector's — to hold the token for the short span between the two calls. If a caller presents no token, or an expired one, `/identity` fails closed with a generic error that does not distinguish \"no token\" from \"bad token\" from \"vendor rejected it\" (same user-enumeration discipline as §4's other failure-handling rules)."
